{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.35 -7.96 M 4.33 -5.77 C 3.63 -5.46 2.63 -6.19 2.1 -7.4 C 1.56 -8.61 1.68 -9.84 2.38 -10.15 C 3.07 -10.46 4.07 -9.73 4.61 -8.52 C 5.15 -7.31 5.02 -6.08 4.33 -5.77 Z M 3.35 -7.96}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.86 -6.69 M 4.17 -6.69 C 4.17 -6.52 4.03 -6.38 3.86 -6.38 C 3.69 -6.38 3.55 -6.52 3.55 -6.69 C 3.55 -6.87 3.69 -7.01 3.86 -7.01 C 4.03 -7.01 4.17 -6.87 4.17 -6.69 Z M 3.86 -6.69}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}T}(\varphi)
for

\varphi
. By Lemma
3
, this happens when

\displaystyle\frac{\cosh(\hat{r})-\cosh(r(S))}{\sinh(\hat{r})-\sinh(r(S))\cos(\varphi-\varphi(S))}=\frac{\cosh(\hat{r})-\cosh(r(T))}{\sinh(\hat{r})-\sinh(r(T))\cos(\varphi-\varphi(T))}.

Note that solving this equation for
\varphi
is equivalent to
finding the roots of the function

f(\varphi)=a\cos(\varphi-\varphi(S))-b\cos(\varphi-\varphi(T))+c
,
where the constants
a,b
, and
c
are defined as

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\left(\cosh(\hat{r})-\cosh(r(T))\right)\sinh(r(S)),~\text{and}~b=\left(\cosh(\hat{r})-\cosh(r(S))\right)\sinh(r(T)),~\text{and}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=\left(\cosh(r(T))-\cosh(r(S))\right)\sinh(\hat{r}).

Note that
f(\varphi)
is the sum of two differently phased cosine
functions of equal frequency with amplitudes
a
and
b
,
respectively, together with a constant
c
. Since the sum of two
sinusoids of the same frequency is another sinusoid, we can use

[
24
, Equation (6)]
, to conclude that

f(\varphi)=a^{\prime}\cos(\varphi+\xi)+c
,
where the constants
a^{\prime}
and
\xi
are given by

\displaystyle a^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left(a\cos(\varphi(S))+b\cos(\varphi(T))\right)^{2}+\left(a\sin(\varphi(S))+b\sin(\varphi(T))\right)^{2}},

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\atan\left(\frac{a\sin(\varphi(S))+b\sin(\varphi(T))}{a\cos(\varphi(S))+b\cos(\varphi(T))}\right),

and
a,b,
and
c
are defined as above. Consequently,

f(\varphi)
is a cosine function with amplitude
a^{\prime}
and period

2\pi
, which has at most two roots in
[0,2\pi)
if
a^{\prime}\neq 0
.
Therefore, it remains to show that
a^{\prime}
is non-zero. First note
that
a^{\prime}
only vanishes if both sums in the quadratic functions do.
Since there exists no
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\cos(x)=0

and

\sin(x)=0
, it follows that
a^{\prime}
is non-zero as long
as
a
and
b
are non-zero. Since
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

are non-degenerate, we know that
r(S),r(T)<\hat{r}
, and
since
\cosh(x)
is strictly increasing for

, we have

\displaystyle\cosh(\hat{r})-\cosh(r(S))>0~\text{and}~\cosh(\hat{r})-\cosh(r(T))>0.

Moreover, since
r(S),r(T)>0
by assumption, it follows that

\sinh(r(S)),\sinh(r(T))>0
, which concludes the proof of the
non-degenerate case.

, since both sites are assumed to be
distinct. In that case, the two ellipses

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

and

\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

intersect only in the pole.
∎

We continue by investigating how the intersections move as the sweep
circle expands. To this end, we first show that the beach ellipses
expand as well.

Lemma 8
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\leq\hat{r}}
be a site and let
r_{1},r_{2}
be two radii
with
r(S)\leq r_{1}<r_{2}
. Then,
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S
at
\hat{r}=r_{1}
is inside of
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S
at
\hat{r}=r_{2}
.

Proof.

Consider a point
P\in\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S
as
\hat{r}=r_{1}
. Then,
the distance between
P
and the sweep circle is given by

r_{1}-r(P)
and is equal to
|\overline{PS}|
. At

\hat{r}=r_{2}
,
|\overline{PS}|
remains unchanged. However,
then the distance between
P
and the sweep circle increases to

r_{2}-r(P)>r_{1}-r(P)=|\overline{PS}|
. It follows that
P
is
closer to
S
than to the sweep circle at
\hat{r}=r_{2}
and
is therefore inside
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S
.
∎

We are now ready to show that the two beach ellipse intersections of a
pair of sites
S
and
T
start at the same point and move along the
bisector
S\!\perp\!T
in opposite directions as the radius

\hat{r}
of the sweep circle increases.

Lemma 9
.

Let
S\neq T\in\mathcal{S}_{\leq\hat{r}}
be two sites with
r(S)\leq r(T)

and consider the intersections

\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}T
and the point

. For

, we have

. For

,

and

are on opposite sides of

\overleftrightarrow{OT}
. As
\hat{r}
increases, so do

and

.

, the beach ellipse

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}T

.
By Lemma

, the two ellipses

\lxSVG@closescope

and
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

and has by definition equal distance to
S
and

T
, i.e., it lies on
S\!\perp\!T
. Moreover, this point is
unique, as
\overline{OT}\not\subset S\!\perp\!T
, since

T\in\overline{OT}
but
T\notin S\!\perp\!T
.

\overleftrightarrow{OT}

but

does not. (The
proof for
|\overline{PI^{\prime}}|
is analogous.) Then

I_{r_{2}}\in\overline{PI_{r_{1}}}
. However, since the beach
ellipse

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

at
\hat{r}=r_{1}
is completely
contained in

at
\hat{r}=r_{2}

(Lemma
8
), it follows that

is inside of

,
contradicting the fact that

.
∎

The above lemma has some interesting implications. Consider two sites

S,T
that are incident to a Voronoi vertex
V
, meaning they lie on
the arc of the witness circle of
V
. Then,
V
lies on the bisector

S\!\perp\!T
and, in particular, on one side of the point

P\in S\!\perp\!T
defined in the lemma. As the sweep circle
expands, the intersections
I
and
I^{\prime}
move along the bisector and
away from
P
, meaning exactly one of them reaches
V
eventually.
More precisely, the following lemma captures from which directions the
intersections approach a Voronoi vertex. Recall that

\sphericalangle ABC
denotes the angle between
\overrightarrow{BA}
and

\overrightarrow{BC}
in clockwise direction around
B
.

Lemma 10
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{V}
be a Voronoi vertex with far point
V^{\prime}
and
incidence tuple
(S_{1},S_{2},S_{3})
. Further, let
(S,S^{\prime})\in\{(S_{1},S_{2}),(S_{2},S_{3}),(S_{1},S_{3})\}
, let
\overrightarrow{V}
be the
angular bisector of
\sphericalangle SVS^{\prime}
, and let
I\in\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S^{\prime}
be the intersection with

I=V
at
\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
. Then,
I\in\overrightarrow{V}
for

\hat{r}<r(V^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Illustration of the proof of
Lemma
10
. The sites
S
and
S^{\prime}

lie on the witness circle of
V
. The intersection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S^{\prime}
that moves
towards
V
lies on the angular bisector
\overrightarrow{V}

(orange) of the angle
\sphericalangle SVS^{\prime}
, since
\overrightarrow{V}
,

I,P
, and
S
are on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
.

Note that
V
divides
S\!\perp\!S^{\prime}
into the two rays

\overrightarrow{V}
and
S\!\perp\!S^{\prime}\setminus\overrightarrow{V}
and
so does the line
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
. Thus, since

I\in S\!\perp\!S^{\prime}
, it suffices to show that
I
and

\overrightarrow{V}
are on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
. Without loss
of generality, assume
r(S)\geq r(S^{\prime})
. By
Lemma
9
there is a point

P=\overline{OS}\cap S\!\perp\!S^{\prime}
such that

I\in\overline{PV}\subset S\!\perp\!S^{\prime}
. Thus,
P
and

I
are on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
. Moreover, since

P\in\overline{OS}
, we also know that
S
and
I
are on the
same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
. Consequently, it suffices to show that

\overrightarrow{V}
is on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
as
S
, as
shown in Figure
9
.

If
S^{\prime}
is on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
as
S
, then this is
trivially true, since
\overrightarrow{V}
is the angular bisector of

\sphericalangle SVS^{\prime}
. Thus, let
S
and
S^{\prime}
lie on opposite sides of

\overleftrightarrow{OV}
and consider the angles
\varphi_{S}=\sphericalangle SVO

and
\varphi_{S^{\prime}}=\sphericalangle OVS^{\prime}
. Since
\overrightarrow{V}
is the
angle bisector of
\sphericalangle SVS^{\prime}
, we know that the angle between

\overrightarrow{VS}
and
\overrightarrow{V}
is

1/2(\varphi_{S}+\varphi_{S^{\prime}})
. To show that
\overrightarrow{V}
is
on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OV}
as
S
, it then suffices to show
that
1/2(\varphi_{S}+\varphi_{S^{\prime}})\leq\varphi_{S}
or equivalently
that
\varphi_{S^{\prime}}\leq\varphi_{S}
. To this end, we make use of
the hyperbolic law of cosines.

Consider the triangles
\bigtriangleup OVS
and

\bigtriangleup OVS^{\prime}
and note that
\varphi_{S}
and
\varphi_{S^{\prime}}

are the angles at
V
, respectively. Moreover, since
S
and
S^{\prime}

lie on the witness circle of
V
, we have

|\overline{VS}|=|\overline{VS^{\prime}}|
. If
r(S^{\prime})=r(S)
, the side lengths
of the triangles match and we have
\varphi_{S}=\varphi_{S^{\prime}}
by
Equation (
1
). If
r(S^{\prime})<r(S)
, i.e., if

|\overline{OS^{\prime}}|<|\overline{OS}|
, we can apply
Lemma
1
to conclude that

\varphi_{S^{\prime}}<\varphi_{S}
.
∎

As a corollary of the above lemma, we can conclude that before the
sweep circle radius reaches the far point
V^{\prime}
of
V
, there are two
intersections whose distance to the sites of the intersecting beach
ellipses is smaller than to the third site, as shown in
Figure
10
.

Figure 10:
Illustration of Corollary
11
.
The distance from
I
to
S^{\prime}
is smaller than the one to
S^{\prime\prime}
.

.

and

. Further, let

and consider

I=V
at
\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
. Then,

|\overline{IS^{\prime\prime}}|>|\overline{IS}|=|\overline{IS^{\prime}}|
for

\hat{r}<r(V^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that

\sphericalangle S^{\prime}VS^{\prime\prime}\leq\sphericalangle S^{\prime\prime}VS
. Consider the two
triangles
\bigtriangleup IVS^{\prime}
and
\bigtriangleup IVS^{\prime\prime}
, as
illustrated in Figure
10
, and note that

|\overline{VS^{\prime}}|=|\overline{VS^{\prime\prime}}|
, since
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
lie on the
witness circle of
V
. By Lemma
10
,
we know that
I
lies on the angle bisector
\overrightarrow{V}
of

\sphericalangle SVS^{\prime}
, meaning
I
lies between
\overrightarrow{VS}
and

\overrightarrow{VS^{\prime}}
in clockwise direction around
V
. By definition of
the incidence tuple, we know that
S^{\prime\prime}
does not lie between

\overrightarrow{VS}
and
\overrightarrow{VS^{\prime}}
in clockwise direction, meaning

\sphericalangle IVS^{\prime}<\sphericalangle IVS^{\prime\prime}
. Consequently, we can apply
Lemma
1
to conclude that

|\overline{IS}|=|\overline{IS^{\prime}}|<|\overline{IS^{\prime\prime}}|
.
∎

Finally, we investigate how we can use the beach ellipse intersections
to predict circle events and how to distinguish between true and false
ones (see Section

). Predicting the event is
straightforward, as we only need to compute the intersection
P
of
the two bisectors corresponding to two beach ellipse intersections
that are consecutive
4

4

18
that it suffices to
consider consecutive intersections.
on the beach curve. We note
that
P

If it does exist, we need to determine whether the two beach ellipse
intersections converge towards

as the sweep circle expands. To

and

and

P=I=I^{\prime}
at
\hat{r}=r(P)+d
, if
and only if
P
and
I
(resp.
I^{\prime}
) are on the same side of

\overleftrightarrow{OS}
(resp.
\overleftrightarrow{OS^{\prime}}
) at
\hat{r}=r
.

Proof.

We give the proof for
I
and
\overleftrightarrow{OS}
. The one for
I^{\prime}
and

\overleftrightarrow{OS^{\prime}}
is analogous. Note that the positions of the sites
and thus the coordinates of
S
and
P
are fixed. Consequently,

P
is on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OS}
at all times. Moreover, by
Lemma
9
the intersection
I
is on the
same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OS}
at all times. It follows that, if and
only if
P
and
I
are on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OS}
at a given
sweep circle radius, then this holds for all sweep circle radii.

When
\hat{r}=r(P)+d
, the sweep circle has equal distance
to
P
as to all sites
S_{1},S_{2}
, and
S_{3}
, meaning
P
lies on
their beach ellipses. In particular, we have
P=I
. Clearly,
P

and
I
lie on the same side of
\overleftrightarrow{OS}
at that point. By the
above argumentation, then and only then does the same hold at

\hat{r}=r
.
∎

12
.

. The
point

are on the same sides
of
\overleftrightarrow{OS}
and
\overleftrightarrow{OS^{\prime}}
, respectively.

(Right)
The intersections meet at
P
when

\hat{r}=r(P)+d
.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S^{\prime}
. Since

I\in\mathcal{B}
, we know that
r(I)\geq r(P)
, meaning
I
is not
inside the beach ellipse
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S^{\prime}
. Thus,
I
is at least as
close to the sweep circle as to
S^{\prime}
. Since the distance between

I
and the sweep circle is given by
\hat{r}-r(I)
, it
follows that

|\overline{S^{\prime}I}|\geq\hat{r}-r(I)=|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|
.

It remains to consider the case where
I\notin\mathcal{B}
. Then,
there exists a beach ellipse segment of another site
S^{\prime}\neq S,T

that is active at angular coordinate
\varphi(I)
. That is, there
is a point
P=\overrightarrow{OI}\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S^{\prime}
such that

r(I)<r(P)
. It follows that
I
is inside of
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S^{\prime}
,
meaning
I
is closer to
S^{\prime}
than to the sweep circle. We can
conclude that

|\overline{S^{\prime}I}|<\hat{r}-r(I)=|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|
.
∎

With the above lemma, we are now ready to investigate how changes to
the beach curve are related to the events in the queue
\mathcal{Q}
.
Consider the tuple
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
containing all site events and all
true circle events (see Section
3.2
). Recall that

V^{\prime}
denotes the far point of a Voronoi vertex
V
, i.e., the point
with the maximum radial coordinate among points on the witness circle
of
V
. Then,
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}=(r_{0},r_{1},\dots,r_{k},r_{k+1})

contains the radii
r_{0}=0
,
r_{k+1}=\infty
and all radii

\{r(S)\mid S\in\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{r(V^{\prime})\mid V\in\mathcal{V}\}
in
ascending order inbetween. We note that
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
is different
from
\mathcal{Q}
, since the latter also contains structure events and
circle events that are later canceled, e.g., when a site is detected
within the corresponding witness circle.

For two sites
S,T\in\mathcal{S}_{\leq\hat{r}}
, we say that a point

P\in\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope

enters the beach
curve at radius
r
, if there is an
\varepsilon>0
such that

P\notin\mathcal{B}
for
\hat{r}\in[r-\varepsilon,r)
and

P\in\mathcal{B}
when
\hat{r}=r
. Analogously, we say that

P\in\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}T

leaves the beach
curve at radius

r
if
P\in\mathcal{B}
when
\hat{r}=r
and
there is an
\varepsilon>0
such that
P\notin\mathcal{B}
for

\hat{r}\in(r,r+\varepsilon]
. In the following, we show
that no beach ellipse intersection enters or leaves the beach curve
between two events in
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
.

Lemma 14
.

Let
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
be the tuple of site events and true circle
events and let
r_{i},r_{j}\in\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
be two consecutive
events. Then, for all
\hat{r}\in(r_{i},r_{j})
no beach
ellipse intersection enters the beach curve.

Proof.

Let
S,T
be two sites and let

I\in\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}T
be an intersection
of their ellipses. For the sake of contradiction, assume that there
exists an
r\in(r_{i},r_{j})
such that
I
enters the beach curve at

r
. Note that no site event occurs in
(r_{i},r_{j})
, since this
would contradict the construction of
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
. Consequently,
if
I
is on the beach curve at
\hat{r}=r
, we know that
I

is contained in the sweep circle for all

\hat{r}\in(r_{i},r_{j})
. By
Lemma
13
we know that
I
not being on
the beach curve before
\hat{r}=r
implies the existence of
at least one site
S^{\prime}\neq S,T
, such that

|\overline{S^{\prime}I}|<|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|
. In particular, we
choose
S^{\prime}
to be the one that remains active the longest at angular
coordinate
\varphi(I)
as the sweep circle expands beyond
r_{i}
.
Since
I
is on the beach curve at
\hat{r}=r
, we also know
that
|\overline{S^{\prime}I}|\geq|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|
at that moment
(again Lemma
13
). Thus, as
I
moves
along the bisector
S\!\perp\!T
, there exists an

r^{\prime}\in(r_{i},r]
such that

|\overline{S^{\prime}I}|=|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|

(Lemma
2
). Then,
I
is the center of
an empty circle (as otherwise there would be yet another site that
is longer active than
S^{\prime}
, contradicting the choice of
S^{\prime}
) and
thus lies on a Voronoi vertex
V\in\mathcal{V}
. However, this would
imply that
r^{\prime}\in(r_{i},r_{j})
is the radius of the far point of

V
, which again contradicts the construction of
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
.
∎

Note that if no intersections enter the beach curve between events in

\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
, then also no beach ellipse segments can become active
then. Moreover, no segments become active during a circle event
either, as only two intersections are merged into one there. We can
conclude the following lemma, which is the hyperbolic sweep circle
counterpart of
[
11
, Lemma 7.6]
in the Euclidean sweep
line version.

Lemma 15
.

The only way in which a new beach ellipse segment can become active
is through a site event.

We continue by investigating how beach ellipse segments disappear from
the beach curve. Analogous to the proof of
Lemma
14
, we can prove that no beach
ellipse intersection leaves the beach curve between two events in

\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
.

Lemma 16
.

Let
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
be the tuple of site events and true circle
events and let
r_{i},r_{j}\in\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
be two consecutive
events. Then, for all
\hat{r}\in(r_{i},r_{j})
no beach
ellipse intersection leaves the beach curve.

Proof.

Consider two sites
S,T
, let

I\in\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}T
be an intersection
of their ellipses, and assume for the sake of contradiction that
there exists an
r\in(r_{i},r_{j})
such that
I
leaves the beach
curve at
r
. That is, there exists an
\varepsilon>0
, such that

I
is on the beach curve until
\hat{r}=r
and is no longer
on the beach curve for
\hat{r}\in(r,r+\varepsilon)
. By
Lemma
13
, it follows that for all sites

S^{\prime}\neq S,T
we have
|\overline{S^{\prime}I}|\geq|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|

at
\hat{r}=r
, but at least one of them is closer to
I

than
|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|
afterwards. Let
S^{\prime\prime}
be the one
for which this happens first. That is, for this site we have

|\overline{S^{\prime\prime}I}|\geq|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|
at
\hat{r}=r

and
|\overline{S^{\prime\prime}I}|<|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{ST}|
afterwards. By
Lemma
2
, we know that when
I
moves
along the bisector
S\!\perp\!T
as the sweep circle expands,
there exists a radius
r^{\prime}\in[r,r+\varepsilon)
such that

|\overline{S^{\prime\prime}I}|=|\overline{SI}|=|\overline{TI}|
when

\hat{r}=r^{\prime}
. As by the choice of
S^{\prime\prime}
, no other site is
closer to
I
than
S^{\prime\prime},S
, and
T
, we know that
I
is the center
of an empty circle and thus lies on a Voronoi vertex

V\in\mathcal{V}
. However, this implies that
r^{\prime}\in(r_{i},r_{j})
is
the radius of the far point of
V
, which contradicts the
construction of
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
.
∎

Again, note that if no intersections can leave the beach curve between
events in
\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
, then also no beach ellipse segment can
become inactive then. Moreover, no segments become inactive during a
site event, since we only insert two intersections consecutively on
the beach curve there. As a result, we obtain the following lemma,
which is the hyperbolic equivalent of
[
11
, Lemma 7.7]
in
the Euclidean sweep line approach.

Lemma 17
.

The only way in which an active beach ellipse segment can become
inactive is through a circle event.

A.3.3
Voronoi Vertices

It remains to prove that all Voronoi vertices are actually found by
means of circle events. The following lemma is the analog
of
[
11
, Lemma 7.8]
in the Euclidean version. To simplify
the following proof, we assume that at most three sites are incident
to a Voronoi vertex
V
. If this does not hold, the algorithm may
produce duplicate Voronoi vertices, which need to be merged in a
post-processing step.

Lemma 18
.

Every Voronoi vertex is detected by means of a circle event.

Proof.

Let
V\in\mathcal{V}
be a Voronoi vertex, let
V^{\prime}
be its far point,
and let
(S_{1},S_{2},S_{3})
be the incidence tuple of
V
. Further,
let
r\in\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
be the predecessor of
r(V^{\prime})
in

\mathcal{\bar{Q}}
. We prove that for all sweep circle radii

\hat{r}\in[r,r(V^{\prime}))
there are beach ellipse intersections

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S_{1}\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

and

I_{23}\in\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S_{2}\cap\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S_{3}
that are
consecutive on the beach curve
\mathcal{B}
. Then, it follows that the
corresponding circle event is in
\mathcal{Q}
and the Voronoi vertex

V
is detected at
\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
.

We start by showing that
I_{12},I_{23}\in\mathcal{B}
for

\hat{r}\in[r,r(V^{\prime}))
. In particular, we give the proof for

I_{12}
, as the one for
I_{23}
is analogous. For the sake of
contradiction, assume that there exists an
r^{\prime}\in[r,r(V^{\prime}))
such
that
I_{12}\notin\mathcal{B}
when
\hat{r}=r^{\prime}
. By
Lemma
7
,
I_{12}
exists, so the only
way for it
not
to be on the beach curve is that there exists
another site
S\neq S_{1},S_{2}
such that

|\overline{SI_{12}}|<|\overline{S_{1}I_{12}}|=|\overline{S_{2}I_{12}}|

(Lemma
13
). Moreover, by
Corollary
11
we have

|\overline{S_{3}I_{12}}|>|\overline{S_{1}I_{12}}|=|\overline{S_{1}I_{12}}|
. It
follows that
S\neq S_{3}
. We now show that the site
S
that is
distinct from
S_{1},S_{2}
, and
S_{3}
cannot exist.

By Lemma
14
we know that
I_{12}
does
not enter the beach curve until at least
\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
.
Now first consider the case where
I_{12}
enters the beach curve
exactly at
\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
, i.e., exactly when
I_{12}=V
.
This means that

|\overline{SI_{12}}|\geq|\overline{S_{1}I_{12}}|=|\overline{S_{2}I_{12}}|
at
this point (Lemma
13
). In particular, we
have
|\overline{SI_{12}}|=|\overline{S_{1}I_{12}}|=|\overline{S_{2}I_{12}}|

then, as by Lemma
2
there exists a point
at which we have equality but this point does not occur before

\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
. It follows that besides
S_{1},S_{2}
, and

S_{3}
, the site
S
is on the witness circle of
V
, contradicting
our assumption that no more than three sites do. Now consider the
case where
I_{12}
does not enter the beach curve at

\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
, i.e., when
I_{12}=V
. By
Lemma
13
, we know that

|\overline{SI_{12}}|<|\overline{S_{1}I_{12}}|
, i.e,

|\overline{SV}|<|\overline{S_{1}V}|
, which means that
S
is contained in
the witness circle of
V
, contradicting the assumption that
V
is
a Voronoi vertex. Since both cases lead to a contradiction, we can
conclude that
I_{12}\in\mathcal{B}
for all

\hat{r}\in[r,r(V^{\prime}))
.

It remains to show that
I_{12}
and
I_{23}
are also consecutive
on
\mathcal{B}
for
\hat{r}\in[r,r(V^{\prime}))
. First note that any
intersection that were to lie between
I_{12}
and
I_{23}
cannot
span beyond these two intersections, as this would contradict the
fact that
I_{12},I_{23}\in\mathcal{B}
, which we just proved. It
follows that if
I_{12},I_{23}
are not consecutive on
\mathcal{B}
,
then there are at least two intersections
I
and
I^{\prime}
between them
that belong to the same active segment
\mathcal{A}
, which is part of
the beach ellipse of another site
S
. Since none of the
intersections
I_{12},I_{23},I
, and
I^{\prime}
leave
\mathcal{B}
until at
least
\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
(Lemma
16
),
it follows that
I
and
I^{\prime}
stay between
I_{12}
and
I_{23}

until
\hat{r}=r(V^{\prime})
, which is when
I_{12}
and
I_{23}

meet at
V
. Then,
V=I_{12}=I_{23}=I=I^{\prime}
. Now note that
only three intersections of the beach ellipses of the sites

S_{1},S_{2}
, and
S_{3}
meet at
V
, since the two beach ellipse
intersections of a pair of them travel in opposite directions on the
perpendicular bisector, only one of which leads to
V

(Lemma
9
). Thus, at least one of
I
and

I^{\prime}
belongs to a beach ellipse
\hbox to5.56pt{\vbox to6.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.77756pt\lower-3.4843pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.02 4.54 C 0.67 5.15 -1.33 3.6 -2.45 1.09 C -3.57 -1.42 -3.38 -3.94 -2.02 -4.54 C -0.67 -5.15 1.33 -3.6 2.45 -1.09 C 3.57 1.42 3.38 3.94 2.02 4.54 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 2.68 M 1.64 2.68 C 1.64 3.01 1.37 3.28 1.04 3.28 C 0.71 3.28 0.45 3.01 0.45 2.68 C 0.45 2.35 0.71 2.09 1.04 2.09 C 1.37 2.09 1.64 2.35 1.64 2.68 Z M 1.04 2.68}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}S
with

S\neq S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
. It follows that a fourth site lies on the
witness circle of
V
, contradicting the assumption that no more
than three do.
∎

A.4
Complexity

To conclude the proof of Theorem
5
, it remains to show
that the algorithm takes time
\mathcal{O}(n\log(n))
to compute the
Voronoi diagram of
n
sites. Initially, all site events need to be
scheduled, meaning the sites have to be sorted by their radii, which
takes time
\mathcal{O}(n\log(n))
. The running time of the
remainder of the algorithm then depends on the complexity of the
diagram, i.e., the number of Voronoi vertices and edges. It was
previously shown that this complexity is
\mathcal{O}(n)
, by
examining different models: the Poincare disk model
[
6
, Consequence
of Proposition 2]
, the Poincaré
half-plane model
[
10
, Theorem 18.5.1]
and the Klein disk
model
[
27
, Theorem 1]
. Of course, it is no
surprise that all came to the same conclusion, since the different
models represent different ways to address points in the same space.

Clearly, there are exactly
n
site events and the number of true
circle events is bounded by the number of Voronoi vertices, which is

\mathcal{O}(n)
. As each event is processed, at most a constant
number of circle events are scheduled and as the algorithm proceeds
the number of canceled events cannot be larger than the scheduled
ones. Moreover, since each intersection can contribute at most one
structure event, it follows that the total number of processed events
is
\mathcal{O}(n)
.

It remains to show that we can handle an event in time

\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
. Since the queue contains
\mathcal{O}(n)

events, inserting and removing elements from the queue, takes time

\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
. Regarding updating the beach curve data
structure, recall that two beach ellipses intersect at most two times
(Lemma
7
) and note that, consequently,
at most two intersections are on the same edge of the Voronoi diagram
at all times. It follows that the number of elements in the beach
curve is at most
\mathcal{O}(n)
at all times, meaning insertions and
deletions take at most
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
time. All other
operations, like inserting vertices to the diagram, marking bisectors
incident to the vertices, and predicting new circle and structure
events, take constant time.
